High-dimensional learning dynamics of multi-pass Stochastic Gradient Descent in multi-index models
Zhou Fan, Leda Wang

Abstract
. We study the learning dynamics of a multi-pass, mini-batch Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) procedure for empirical risk minimization in high-dimensional multi-index models with isotropic random data. In an asymptotic regime where the sample size n n and data dimension d d increase proportionally, for any sub-linear batch size κ ≍ n α \kappa\asymp n^{\alpha} where α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in[0,1) , and for a commensurate “critical” scaling of the learning rate, we provide an asymptotically exact characterization of the coordinate-wise dynamics of SGD. This characterization takes the form of a system of dynamical mean-field equations, driven by a scalar Poisson jump process that represents the asymptotic limit of SGD sampling noise. We develop an analogous characterization of the Stochastic Modified Equation (SME) which provides a Gaussian diffusion approximation to SGD. Our analyses imply that the limiting dynamics for SGD are the same for any batch size scaling α ∈ [ 0 , 1 ) \alpha\in[0,1) , and that under a commensurate scaling of the learning rate, dynamics of SGD, SME, and gradient flow are mutually distinct, with those of SGD and SME coinciding in the special case of a linear model. We recover a known dynamical mean-field characterization of gradient flow in a limit of small learning rate, and of one-pass/online SGD in a limit of increasing sample size n / d → ∞ n/d\to\infty .



1.
Introduction

Introduced by Robbins and Monro in
[
74
]
, Stochastic Gradient
Descent (SGD) and its variants have played an important role in machine
learning, and are often the optimization methods of choice in large-scale
learning systems
[
16
,
15
]
. Existing theory for SGD explains many aspects
of its behavior, including optimization theory on convergence rates for
convex optimization problems
[
67
,
66
,
72
,
7
]
, classical formulations of
ODE scaling limits under small learning rate asymptotics

[
56
,
49
]
, and
analyses of diffusion approximations, especially near critical points and
minimizers of the loss landscape

[
57
,
52
,
34
,
43
,
11
,
54
]
.
Such results are suggestive of a behavior that is similar to (non-stochastic)
gradient descent and gradient flow,
with additional diffusivity properties near criticality.
However, in many modern examples of learning systems — often
characterized by a high-dimensional optimization parameter, a complex and
highly non-convex loss landscape, and a combination of small batch size and
large learning rate — it is also empirically observed that SGD may have
markedly different properties from gradient flow, possibly leading to improved
generalization
[
47
,
44
]
. Our understanding
of SGD in such contexts remains incomplete.

In this work, we will study the dynamics of SGD in a prototypical
high-dimensional application of empirical risk minimization for a multi-index
model, with isotropic random data
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
and labels

y=\sigma^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon),\qquad\bm{\theta}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k^{*}}.

Given
n
such data observations
(\mathbf{x}_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{x}_{n},y_{n})
, we will
consider a standard mini-batch, multi-pass SGD procedure for optimizing a
(possibly non-convex) empirical risk with coordinate-separable regularizer,

(1)

\mathcal{R}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}),y_{i})+\sum_{j=1}^{d}G(\theta_{j}),\qquad\bm{\theta}=[\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{d}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}.

Our main result, building upon the method of
[
18
]
, will
provide an asymptotically exact characterization of the
learning dynamics of SGD in this setting, with a general sub-linear batch
size
\kappa\ll n
, and under a high-dimensional asymptotic limit
as
n,d\to\infty
proportionally.

Our analysis is motivated and inspired by three lines of related literature:

1.

A large body of work, starting with

[
77
,
76
,
73
,
10
]
and the mathematical
formalizations of such findings in
[
88
,
37
]
,
has investigated these learning dynamics in an
analogous
one-pass
or
online learning
setting, where a new
sample
(\mathbf{x}_{i},y_{i})
is used in each SGD iteration.

The resulting dynamics are characterized in the high-dimensional limit by a system of ODEs for the overlap parameters

d^{-1}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{*})^{\top}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+k^{*})\times(k+k^{*})}
. Diffusive behavior near critical points was further studied in

[
5
]
; escape from initial conditions and saddle points
over long time horizons in problems with symmetry has been the subject of
intensive investigation in

[
35
,
33
,
4
,
8
,
85
,
1
,
27
,
26
,
46
]
;
extensions to two-layer neural networks with more general dimension scalings, and connections to dynamics in the “mean-field” limit of infinite width
(
[
61
,
21
,
75
]
), were discussed
in
[
86
,
2
]
; and extensions to non-isotropic
data and adaptive dynamics were obtained
in
[
22
,
23
]
.

Such results have illuminated how classical ODE and SDE
approximations of SGD may pertain to high-dimensional settings, and
how problem symmetries may affect the sample complexity of learning. They
shed light also on the dynamics of early training in multi-pass SGD, where
initial iterations are akin to online learning without sample re-use.
However, the full learning dynamics of multi-pass SGD
differs from that of online SGD in later training, and understanding these
differences may be requisite for investigating the optimization of the empirical
— rather than population — risk, and associated issues of overfitting
versus generalization.

2.

Using statistical physics techniques of dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT)

[
83
,
84
,
24
,
25
,
6
,
3
]
, a burgeoning body of literature has developed asymptotically exact
characterizations of multi-pass gradient-based algorithms in a variety
of statistical learning models

[
80
,
58
,
78
,
79
,
81
,
55
]
, with
substantial recent interest in the training dynamics for various neural network
architectures

[
14
,
12
,
13
,
65
,
39
,
60
]
.
Closest to our current analyses is the work of
[
62
,
63
,
64
]
which characterized the
high-dimensional limit of discrete-time SGD dynamics with a linear batch
size
\kappa\asymp n
per iteration, as well as a “persistent SGD” dynamics
in continuous time that maintains this batch size while replacing samples
individually. Investigations of differences between such dynamics and gradient
flow in a model of phase retrieval were
carried out in
[
63
]
. Differences between the sample complexities of
one-pass and multi-pass methods for learning with
symmetries were also highlighted in
[
50
,
29
]
.

The pioneering work of
[
18
]
developed a new approach
to mathematically formalize such DMFT characterizations, which previously
were often derived
using non-rigorous techniques. This work
[
18
]
established on rigorous
grounds the high-dimensional limiting dynamics of gradient flow for a
class of multi-index models. Several extensions to other
discrete-time and continuous-time first-order optimization and sampling methods have since been developed

[
36
,
40
,
30
,
31
,
41
,
20
,
19
,
28
]
, including analyses
of discrete-time SGD with linear batch size
\kappa\asymp n
in
[
36
]

and of Glauber dynamics in the
Sherrington-Kirkpatrick model for batch updates of
\kappa\asymp n
spins in
[
28
]

We remark that a batch size of
\kappa\asymp n
implies limited stochasticity
in the gradient approximation. Motivated by a common belief that the
stochasticity of small-batch dynamics may underlie important differences between
SGD and gradient flow
[
47
,
44
]
, we will extend the
results of
[
18
,
36
]
to (rigorously) characterize the dynamics of SGD for any batch size

\kappa\asymp n^{\alpha},\qquad\alpha\in[0,1),

under a commensurate scaling of the learning rate.
Our results will elucidate how the stochasticity of the gradient approximation
manifests in the limiting dynamics.

3.

An interesting line of work

[
71
,
68
,
51
,
69
,
59
,
70
]
obtained high-dimensional
asymptotic characterizations of single-sample (i.e.
\alpha=0
and
\kappa=1
) multi-pass SGD
and several of its variants, in the setting of a linear regression
model with ridge regularization, and possibly non-isotropic data. One finding of
this work, highlighted in
[
69
,
70
]
, is that the asymptotic dynamics of
quadratic observables of
\bm{\theta}
coincide
with those of a simplified SDE approximation (dubbed “Homogenized
SGD”). Whereas previous analyses establish the accuracy of such
diffusion approximations in the asymptotics of vanishing learning rate

\eta\to 0
for fixed dimensions
d

[
53
,
43
]
, the results of
[
70
]

showed that such a diffusion approximation remains asymptotically
exact under a commonly studied (large) learning rate scaling for a
high-dimensional linear model, raising a tantalizing question of whether this
holds true in more general high-dimensional learning problems.

Restricting to the setting of isotropic data
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we provide a
negative answer to this question, by developing an analogous characterization
of the limiting dynamics of the Stochastic Modified Equation (SME) that was introduced and studied in
[
52
,
53
]

as a diffusion approximation for the SGD process.
Our results illustrate that the asymptotic dynamics of SME and SGD are, in
general, different for multi-index models under commonly studied learning
rate scalings in high-dimensional settings, and they coincide only in the case of linear regression.

1.1.
Summary of results

Our main result provides a mathematical description of the
learning dynamics of a SGD procedure for minimizing the
empirical risk (
1
) over
O(1)
training epochs, in the
high-dimensional limit as
n,d\to\infty
proportionally. This will
take the form of a characterization for the exact limiting value of any
coordinate-separable observables,

\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\psi(\theta_{j}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta_{j}^{t_{m}},\theta_{j}^{*}),\qquad\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\psi(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t_{m}},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*}),

depending on the latent parameter
\bm{\theta}^{*}=[\theta_{1}^{*},\ldots,\theta_{d}^{*}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k^{*}}
and SGD iterates

\bm{\theta}^{t}=[\theta_{1}^{t},\ldots,\theta_{d}^{t}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}

at fixed time points
t_{1},\ldots,t_{m}
in units of training epochs. Such
observables can encompass, for example, the overlap parameters

d^{-1}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{*})^{\top}(\bm{\theta},\bm{\theta}^{*})
and evaluations of the
training and test losses.

As this characterization involves dynamical mean-field theory and
is somewhat complex, we summarize here a few qualitative aspects/implications
of our results:

1.

For any sub-linear choice of batch size

\kappa\asymp n^{\alpha}

with
\alpha\in[0,1)
, and for a commensurate scaling of the learning rate

\eta\asymp n^{\alpha}
,
1
1

1

Our scaling conventions for the model are
\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}\asymp 1
and
\|\bm{\theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\asymp\sqrt{d}
. Under a common alternative scaling of

\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}\asymp\sqrt{d}
and
\|\bm{\theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\asymp 1
, the equivalent
learning rate is
\tilde{\eta}=\eta/d\asymp n^{-1+\alpha}
.
there is a well-defined scaling limit for
the SGD iterates under a time rescaling by
n^{1-\alpha}
,
i.e. to time units of training epochs.

Furthermore, this limit is the same for any choice of
\alpha\in[0,1)
(but
different from the setting of linear batch sizes
\alpha=1
and
\kappa\asymp n
), depending only
on
\bar{\kappa}=\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\kappa/n^{\alpha}
and

\bar{\eta}=\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\eta/n^{\alpha}
. This agrees with previous
empirical observations in more complex models

[
44
,
42
]
that the learning
dynamics of SGD seem to depend moreso on the ratio of learning rate to batch
size than the absolute size of the batch.

2.

The random sampling of mini-batches in SGD leads to a dynamical
mean-field characterization in which a scalar process
\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

that tracks the distributional dynamics of
\{\{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{i\in[n]}\}_{t\geq 0}
is
driven by a univariate Poisson jump process
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.
The presence of this additional Poisson process constitutes the primary
distinction between the asymptotic dynamics of SGD and gradient flow.

For the SME diffusion approximation, the dynamics of
\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
are
analogously driven by a univariate Brownian diffusion with mean and covariance
matching those of
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
.

3.

In a further scaling limit of small learning rate
\bar{\eta}\to 0
(after
taking
n,d\to\infty
with
\eta\approx n^{\alpha}\bar{\eta}
) and
rescaling of time, the dynamics of both
SGD and SME reduce to those of gradient flow. This agrees with the classical
theory on convergence of SGD dynamics to an ODE under small learning rate
asymptotics
[
49
]
, and clarifies that in this high-dimensional context,
the notion of a “small” learning rate under which such an ODE approximation
is accurate may be understood as
\eta\ll n^{\alpha}
. For larger learning rates represented by any
fixed value of
\bar{\eta}=\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\eta/n^{\alpha}>0
, the asymptotic dynamics of SGD, SME, and
gradient flow are, in general, all distinct.

4.

In the special case of linear regression, the
dynamical mean-field equations simplify to deterministic integro-differential
equations for the correlation and response processes, which, in particular,
depend only on the first and second moment statistics of the Poisson
process/Brownian diffusion
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. Thus, in this case, the
dynamics of quadratic observables of SME and SGD coincide even the setting of
fixed
\bar{\eta}>0
, in agreement with the results of

[
70
]
.

5.

Setting
\gamma=\lim_{n,d\to\infty}n/d
, in a further scaling limit
\gamma\to\infty
(after taking
n,d\to\infty
with
n\approx\gamma d
), the dynamical mean-field equations also simplify,
reducing to a Markov diffusion for a scalar process
\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
that tracks the distributional dynamics of
\{\{\theta_{j}^{t}\}_{j\in[d]}\}_{t\geq 0}
. From this simplification, one may recover the known ODE
characterization of the overlap parameters
for one-pass/online SGD
[
77
,
37
]
.

6.

Since the driving Poisson process
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
in the
dynamical mean-field limit for SGD is discrete, with
O_{\mathbb{P}}(1)
jumps over finite
time horizons (in units of training epochs), this enables rapid numerical simulation of
the preceding process
\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
. In applications
where
G(\cdot)
is also a ridge regularizer so that the statistics of the
process
\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
are computable analytically, this leads to a
dynamical mean-field limit for SGD that is more amenable to numerical simulation than its
counterparts for gradient flow or SME.

We will elaborate upon these discussions further in Section
2
,
after formally stating our main results.

1.2.
Notational conventions

For vectors
\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{l}
,
we write
\mathbf{x}\otimes\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times l}
for the outer product. For a
function
g:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
and input
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
, we
write
g(\bm{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
for the application of
g(\cdot)

to each row. We write
\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\mathbf{v}_{i}}
for the empirical
distribution on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
of the rows of

\mathbf{V}=[\mathbf{v}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{v}_{n}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
.

The notation

\|\cdot\|
without subscript will refer to the Euclidean norm
\|\cdot\|_{2}
for
vectors and Frobenius norm
\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{F}}
for matrices. We write

\|\cdot\|_{\mathrm{op}}
for the matrix
\ell_{2}
-to-
\ell_{2}
operator norm, and

\|\cdot\|_{\infty}
for the element-wise
\ell_{\infty}
norm.
\mathrm{Id}_{k}
is the
k\times k
identity matrix, and we omit the subscript when the dimension is
clear. We write
x\lesssim y
if
x\leq Cy
for a constant
C>0
, which does not on the dimensions
n,d
but will usually depend on the fixed
time horizon
T>0
. We write
x\asymp y
if
both
x\lesssim y
and
y\lesssim x
.

2.
Main results

2.1.
Model and dynamics

We consider a standard setting of supervised learning, where

(\mathbf{x}_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{x}_{n},y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}

are
n
independent training observations. It will be assumed that the data

\mathbf{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has independent coordinates with isotropic covariance, and
that the label
y_{i}\in\mathbb{R}
follows a multi-index model, possibly with label
noise: For some parameter
\bm{\theta}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k^{*}}
where
k^{*}\geq 1
,
noise variables

\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n}\in\mathbb{R},

and label map
\sigma^{*}:\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, each label
y_{i}

takes the form

(2)

y_{i}=\sigma^{*}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i}).

Our main result will characterize the training dynamics of a multi-pass
Stochastic Gradient Descent (SGD) algorithm and its Stochastic Modified Equation
(SME) approximant for minimizing a regularized
empirical risk over
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
,

(3)

\mathcal{R}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}L\big(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}),y_{i}\big)+\sum_{j=1}^{d}G(\theta_{j}).

Here
\sigma:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
is a non-linear activation for some
k\geq 1
,
and
L:\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
and
G:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}
are a smooth loss function and regularizer. The gradient of
\mathcal{R}(\bm{\theta})
takes a form

(4)

\nabla\mathcal{R}(\bm{\theta})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}\otimes f(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i})+g(\bm{\theta})\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},

where the functions
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
and

g:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
are defined by

(5)

f(\xi,w^{*},\varepsilon)=L^{\prime}(\sigma(\xi),\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon))\,\nabla\sigma(\xi),\qquad g(\theta)=\nabla G(\theta),

L^{\prime}(\cdot,\cdot)
is the derivative of
L(\cdot,\cdot)
in its first argument,
and
g(\cdot)
is applied row-wise to
\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
.

SGD

The SGD dynamics, initialized at

\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{0}\equiv\bm{\theta}^{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k},

are given by

(6)

\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{t+1}=\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{t}-\eta^{t}\underbrace{\left(\frac{1}{\kappa}\sum_{i\in S^{t}}\mathbf{x}_{i}\otimes f(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i})+\frac{1}{n}\,g(\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{t})\right)}_{:=\mathbf{v}^{t}},\qquad S^{t}\sim\operatorname{Unif}(\mathscr{S}_{\kappa}),

where

\mathscr{S}_{\kappa}=\{S\subset[n]:|S|=\kappa\}

denotes the collection of all subsets of
\{1,\ldots,n\}
of size
\kappa
,
and
\kappa\geq 1
is the batch size.
In each iteration,
\eta^{t}>0
is a learning rate, and

S^{t}\in\mathscr{S}_{\kappa}
is a batch of
\kappa
samples chosen independently and
uniformly at random. The update
\mathbf{v}^{t}
in
(
6
) has expectation
n^{-1}\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}\mathcal{R}(\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{t})

over this randomness of
S^{t}
, and constitutes a stochastic approximation for
this rescaled gradient.

We will study a regime of general sub-linear batch size

\kappa\asymp n^{\alpha},\qquad\alpha\in[0,1),

in a high-dimensional limit where
n,d\to\infty
. (The setting of
\alpha=0

encompasses a constant batch size independent of
n
, including
single-sample SGD with
\kappa=1
.) Then

\frac{n}{\kappa}\asymp n^{1-\alpha}
iterations of the SGD dynamics
(
6
) constitute one “epoch” of training in which, on expectation,
each training sample is used once. We define a continuous-time embedding

\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
of the SGD dynamics (
6
), with time
re-indexed on the scale of epochs, by

(7)

\bm{\theta}^{t}=\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{\lfloor tn^{1-\alpha}\rfloor}.

Our results will characterize the training dynamics of (
7
)
over a fixed number of epochs not depending on
n,d
.

Under our model scaling conventions (to be specified in Assumption

2.2
) where

\|\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{t}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\asymp\sqrt{d}
and
\|\mathbf{x}_{i}\|_{2}\asymp 1
,
we consider a learning rate that scales also as

\eta^{t}\asymp n^{\alpha}.

One may check that this is the scaling under which both the
mean and variance per coordinate of
\eta^{t}\mathbf{v}^{t}
accumulated over one training
epoch has
O(1)
size, and thus non-trivial learning may occur over
O(1)

epochs (in problems without symmetry at initialization). For single-sample SGD where
\alpha=0
and

\kappa=1
, this coincides also with learning rate scalings studied previously
in e.g.
[
37
,
85
,
70
]

(in both online and multi-pass settings).

We formalize the above conditions as the following assumption.

Assumption 2.1

(Batch size and learning rate)
.

There exist constants
\alpha\in[0,1)
,
\bar{\kappa}>0
, and a
Lipschitz-continuous learning rate schedule
\{\bar{\eta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

not depending on
n,d
, such that over each fixed time horizon
T>0
,

\lim_{n,d\to\infty}|\kappa/n^{\alpha}-\bar{\kappa}|=0,\qquad\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}|\eta^{\lfloor tn^{1-\alpha}\rfloor}/n^{\alpha}-\bar{\eta}^{t}|=0.

SME

Under Assumption
2.1
, we define the SME approximation

[
52
]
to the above SGD algorithm as the continuous-time
diffusion process

(8)

\mathrm{d}\bm{\theta}^{t}={-}\bar{\eta}^{t}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{x}_{i}\otimes f(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i})+g(\bm{\theta}^{t})\right)\mathrm{d}t+\frac{\bar{\eta}^{t}}{\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\Big(\mathbf{x}_{i}\otimes f(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i})\Big)\mathrm{d}b_{i}^{t}

where
\{b_{i}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
are independent standard Brownian motions for

i=1,\ldots,n
. More precisely, this may be understood as a diffusion
approximation to the time-rescaled SGD process
\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
in
(
7
), where the drift
and anisotropic diffusion terms of (
8
) are defined to match the mean and
covariance of the stochastic gradient updates defining (
7
).

(In
[
70
]
, the authors study a further “Homogenized
SGD” approximation where each factor

f(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i})
in the diffusion term is
further replaced by

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}f(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i})
. In this
work, we will study instead the diffusion (
8
) which provides a closer
approximation to SGD.)

2.2.
Model assumptions

In addition to Assumption
2.1
on the SGD process, we impose the
following two assumptions on the data model and empirical risk.

Assumption 2.2

(Data model and asymptotic scaling)
.

(a)

As
n,d\to\infty
,
n/d\to\gamma
for a constant
\gamma>0
, and

k,k^{*}\geq 1
are constants not depending on
n,d
.

(b)

(\mathbf{x}_{1},y_{1}),\ldots,(\mathbf{x}_{n},y_{n})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are independent samples.
Each data vector
\mathbf{x}_{i}=(x_{i1},\ldots,x_{id})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
has independent entries
with

\mathbb{E}[x_{ij}]=0,\quad\mathbb{E}[x_{ij}^{2}]=d^{-1},\quad\mathbb{E}[|x_{ij}|^{p}]\leq C_{p}d^{-p/2}\text{ for each $p\geq 3$ and a constant $C_{p}>0$},

and
y_{i}
is given by the multi-index model (
2
).

(c)

The initialization
\bm{\theta}^{0}=[\theta_{1}^{0},\ldots,\theta_{d}^{0}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
, parameter

\bm{\theta}^{*}=[\theta_{1}^{*},\ldots,\theta_{d}^{*}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k^{*}}
,
and noise variables
\bm{\varepsilon}=(\varepsilon_{1},\ldots,\varepsilon_{n})
defining

\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{n})
via (
2
) are non-random and satisfy,
weakly and in Wasserstein-
p
for each fixed order
p\geq 1
as
n,d\to\infty
,

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{(\theta_{j}^{0},\theta_{j}^{*})}\Rightarrow\operatorname{Law}(\theta^{0},\theta^{*}),\qquad\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{\varepsilon_{i}}\Rightarrow\operatorname{Law}(\varepsilon).

Here
\operatorname{Law}(\theta^{0},\theta^{*})
and
\operatorname{Law}(\varepsilon)
are any probability
distributions on
\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}
and
\mathbb{R}
, respectively, that have
finite moment generating functions in a neighborhood of 0.

Assumption
2.2
(c) allows a correlation between
\theta^{0}
and

\theta^{*}
in the limiting law, and thus encompasses settings where the SGD/SME
initialization
\bm{\theta}^{0}
has a non-zero initial overlap with
\bm{\theta}^{*}
.

The assumption that
\bm{\theta}^{0},\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon}
are non-random is only for
convenience of analysis, so that randomness of the data arises entirely through
the data matrix

\mathbf{X}=[\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

We note that our results will then apply equally in settings where

(\bm{\theta}^{0},\bm{\theta}^{*})
and/or
\bm{\varepsilon}
are random and independent of
\mathbf{X}
, upon
applying these results conditioned on
\bm{\theta}^{0},\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon}
.

Assumption 2.3

(Loss and regularizer)
.

Let
f:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
and
g:\mathbb{R}^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
be the
functions defined by (
5
). For a constant
C>0
, these satisfy

\|f(\xi,w^{*},\varepsilon)\|_{2}\leq C,\qquad\|g(\theta)\|_{2}\leq C(1+\|\theta\|_{2}).

Furthermore,
f
and
g
are twice continuously-differentiable,
with all first and second order partial derivatives uniformly bounded by
C
.

These conditions hold, for example, when the activation

\sigma(\cdot)
and loss
L(\cdot,\cdot)
defining the empirical risk
(
3
) are both Lipschitz with
bounded derivatives up to order 3, and the regularizer
G(\cdot)
is
pseudo-Lipschitz with bounded derivatives of orders 2 and 3
(e.g.
G(\theta)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\theta\|_{2}^{2}
).

We expect that our results may be extendable also to pseudo-Lipschitz loss
functions including the squared loss
L(\hat{y},y)=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{y}-y)^{2}
,
under a suitable bound for the learning rate
\{\bar{\eta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

and additional technical arguments. To keep the technicalities simpler,
we will not pursue this extension in our current work.

2.3.
Definitions of the DMFT limit processes

The high-dimensional limit of the dynamics
\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
in
(
7
) or (
8
) is described via a

\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued process
\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
(not depending on
n,d
),
constructed in a probability space of random variables
(\theta^{0},\theta^{*})

that are distributed according to the limit law in Assumption

2.2
(c). Our results will show a convergence of the empirical
distribution of coordinates of any finite-time marginals of

(\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\bm{\theta}^{*})
to the corresponding marginals of the
limit process

(\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\theta^{*})
,

\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{(\theta_{j}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta_{j}^{t_{m}},\theta_{j}^{*})}\Rightarrow\operatorname{Law}(\theta^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta^{t_{m}},\theta^{*}).

Likewise, we will show an analogous convergence of the marginals of
(\{\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon})
to those of a limit process
(\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},w^{*},\varepsilon)
.

In this section, we define these limit processes
\{\theta^{t},\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
for both
SGD and SME, which may be understood as an analogue of the processes described
in
[
18
]
for gradient flow.

2.3.1.
Limit processes for SGD

Fix a time horizon
T>0
. We define the high-dimensional limit
for the time-rescaled SGD process
\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}

of (
7
) via a fixed-point relation
for a system of deterministic correlation and response kernels/operators

(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)
:

•

C_{\theta}
is the joint covariance kernel of a
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued Gaussian
process
\{w^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
and a Gaussian vector
w^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}
.
We denote
C_{\theta}^{t,s}=\mathbb{E}[w^{t}\otimes w^{s}]\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}

for
t,s\in[0,T]
,

C_{\theta}^{t,*}=\mathbb{E}[w^{t}\otimes w^{*}]\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k^{*}}
,
and
C_{\theta}^{*,*}=\mathbb{E}[w^{*}\otimes w^{*}]\in\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}\times k^{*}}
.

•

C_{f}
is the covariance kernel of a
\mathbb{R}^{k}
-valued Gaussian process

\{u^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
. We denote likewise

C_{f}^{t,s}=\mathbb{E}[u^{t}\otimes u^{s}]\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
.

•

R_{\theta}\equiv\{R_{\theta}^{t,s}\}_{t,s\in[0,T]}
is a
\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
-valued process on
[0,T]\times[0,T]
,
where
R_{\theta}^{t,s}=0
if
t<s
.

•

R_{f}\equiv\{R_{f}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
is a process of linear operators,
where
R_{f}^{t}:L^{4}([0,t],\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
for each
t\in[0,T]
.
Given such an operator
R_{f}^{t}
and a matrix-valued process

x\in L^{4}([0,t],\mathbb{R}^{k\times k})
, we will write also

R_{f}^{t}(x)\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
for
R_{f}^{t}
applied column-wise, i.e.

(9)

R_{f}^{t}(x)=[R_{f}^{t}(x_{:,1}),\ldots,R_{f}^{t}(x_{:,k})]

where
x_{:,1},\ldots,x_{:,k}\in L^{4}([0,t],\mathbb{R}^{k})
are the columns of
x
.

•

R_{f}^{*}\equiv\{R_{f}^{t,*}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
is a
\mathbb{R}^{k\times k^{*}}
-valued
process on
[0,T]
.

•

\Gamma\equiv\{\Gamma^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
is a
\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
-valued
process on
[0,T]
.

We defer a specification of further technical conditions for

(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)
to Section

3
.

Given the above objects
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)
, let

(10)

(\theta^{0},\theta^{*})\in\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}},\qquad\varepsilon\in\mathbb{R}

be distributed according to the limit laws of Assumption
2.2
(c),
let

(11)

\{u^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}\sim\operatorname{GP}(0,C_{f}),\quad(\{w^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},w^{*})\sim\operatorname{GP}(0,C_{\theta})

be two centered Gaussian processes with covariance kernels
C_{f},C_{\theta}
, and let

(12)

\{z^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}\sim\operatorname{PP}(\bar{\kappa})

be a
\{0,1,2,\ldots\}
-valued homogeneous Poisson jump process
with rate
\bar{\kappa}
. (Thus
z^{t}\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(\bar{\kappa}t)
marginally for each

t\geq 0
.) We take

(\theta^{0},\theta^{*})
,
\varepsilon
,
\{u^{t}\}
,
(\{w^{t}\},w^{*})
,
and
\{z^{t}\}
to be mutually independent, and
constructed on a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\mathbb{P})
where
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
is a complete and right-continuous
filtration,
\theta^{0},\theta^{*},\varepsilon,w^{*}
are
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable, and

\{u^{t}\},\{w^{t}\},\{z^{t}\}
are
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted and càdlàg (right-continuous
with left limits).

Recalling the limit

\gamma=\lim_{n,d\to\infty}n/d
of Assumption
2.2
(a),
we define the primary DMFT processes
\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

and
\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
as

(13)

\displaystyle\theta^{t}

\displaystyle=\theta^{0}-\int_{0}^{t}\bar{\eta}^{r}\Big({\gamma}\Gamma^{r}\theta^{r}+g(\theta^{r})+{\gamma}R_{f}^{r}(\theta^{[r]})+{\gamma}R_{f}^{r,*}\theta^{*}\Big)\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{\gamma}\,u^{t},

(14)

\displaystyle\xi^{t}

\displaystyle={-}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}f(\xi^{r-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}z^{r}+w^{t},

where we write
x^{[s]}=\{x^{t}\}_{t\in[0,s]}
for the restriction of a process

\{x^{t}\}
to times
t\in[0,s]
, and

x^{t-}=\lim_{s\uparrow t}x^{s}
for any càdlàg process
\{x^{t}\}
.
We define auxiliary response
processes
\{r_{\theta}^{t,s}\}_{t,s\geq 0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}

and
\{r_{f}^{t,*}\}_{t\geq 0}
on
\mathbb{R}^{k\times k^{*}}
by

(15)

\displaystyle r_{\theta}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{k}-\int_{s}^{t}\bar{\eta}^{r}\bigg[\Big({\gamma}\Gamma^{r}+\mathrm{D}g(\theta^{r})\Big)r_{\theta}^{r,s}+{\gamma}R_{f}^{r}(r_{\theta}^{[r],s})\bigg]\mathrm{d}r\text{ for }t\geq s,

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999ptr_{\theta}^{t,s}=0\text{ for }t<s,

(16)

\displaystyle r_{f}^{t,*}

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}r_{f}^{r-,*}\mathrm{d}z^{r}+\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon),

and a process of random linear operators
\{r_{f}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

with
r_{f}^{t}:L^{4}([0,t],\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
by

(17)

\displaystyle r_{f}^{t}(x^{[t]})

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\bigg(r_{f}^{r-}(x^{[r]})+\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{r-},w^{*},\varepsilon)x^{r}\bigg)\mathrm{d}z^{r}.

Here,
\mathrm{D}g(\theta)
denotes the derivative (i.e. Jacobian matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
) of
g(\cdot)
, and similarly

\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi,w^{*},\varepsilon)
and
\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}f(\xi,w^{*},\varepsilon)

denote the derivatives of
f(\cdot)
in
\xi
and
w^{*}
. In
(
15
–
17
),

r_{\theta}^{[r],s}=\{r_{\theta}^{t,s}\}_{t\in[0,r]}
,

R_{f}^{r}(r_{\theta}^{[r],s})
is understood via its application to matrix-valued
processes in (
9
), and
r_{f}^{t-,*}=\lim_{s\uparrow t}r_{f}^{s,*}

and
r_{f}^{t-}(x^{[t]})=\lim_{s\uparrow t}r_{f}^{s}(x^{[s]})
.

The fixed-point relations for

(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)
are then given by

(18)

\displaystyle C_{\theta}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\theta^{t}\otimes\theta^{s}]\text{ for }t,s\in[0,\infty)\cup\{*\}

(19)

\displaystyle R_{\theta}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[r_{\theta}^{t,s}]

(20)

\displaystyle C_{f}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}f(\xi^{r-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}z^{r}\otimes\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}f(\xi^{r-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}z^{r}\Big]

(21)

\displaystyle R_{f}^{t}(x^{[t]})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\big[r_{f}^{t}(x^{[t]})\big]

(22)

\displaystyle R_{f}^{t,*}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[r_{f}^{t,*}]

(23)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{t}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)]

We clarify that in (
21
), the input process
x^{[t]}=\{x^{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]}
is
understood as deterministic, and the expectation is taken with respect to

\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
,
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
,
w^{*}
, and
\varepsilon
defining
(
17
). This yields a deterministic linear operator

R_{f}^{t}:L^{4}([0,t],\mathbb{R}^{k})\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
, which is then applied to the stochastic
inputs in (
13
) and (
15
).

2.3.2.
Limit processes for SME

For the SME diffusion process
\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}

of (
8
), the description of its high-dimensional limit is identical
to the above
equations (
10
–
23
) for SGD, except
with the Poisson jump process
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
in (
12
)
replaced by the scalar Gaussian diffusion process

(24)

z^{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\bar{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\,\mathrm{d}b^{s},

where
\{b^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted standard univariate Brownian
motion. I.e., the Poisson process integrals in (
14
),
(
16
), (
17
), and
(
20
) with respect to
\mathrm{d}z^{r}

are replaced by Wiener integrals with respect to

\bar{\kappa}\,\mathrm{d}r+\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}}\,\mathrm{d}b^{r}
. Note
that the two processes
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
defined by (
12
)
and (
24
) have the same mean and covariance, but differ
otherwise in law.

Remark 2.4

(Forms of the response processes)
.

In the above DMFT systems for both SGD and SME,
one may understand
r_{f}^{t,*}
as a linear response

(25)

r_{f}^{t,*}=\frac{\partial f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial w^{*}},

where (
16
) is computed by
substituting (
14
) in (
25
) and
differentiating in
w^{*}
.

If
C_{f}^{t,s}
were twice continuously-differentiable in
(t,s)
, then
setting
\bar{C}_{f}^{t,s}=\gamma\partial_{t}\partial_{s}C_{f}^{t,s}
, we may write
(
13
) as

(26)

\theta^{t}=\theta^{0}+\int_{0}^{t}\Big[{-}\bar{\eta}^{r}\Big({\gamma}\Gamma^{r}\theta^{r}+g(\theta^{r})+{\gamma}R_{f}^{r}(\theta^{[r]})+{\gamma}R_{f}^{r,*}\theta^{*}\Big)+\bar{u}^{r}\Big]\mathrm{d}r

where
\sqrt{\gamma}\,u^{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\bar{u}^{r}\mathrm{d}r
and
\{\bar{u}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}\sim\operatorname{GP}(0,\bar{C}_{f})
. Then
r_{\theta}^{t,s}
may likewise be understood as the
linear response

(27)

r_{\theta}^{t,s}=\frac{\partial\theta^{t}}{\partial\bar{u}^{s}}

computed by formally differentiating (
26
)
in
\bar{u}^{s}
, and the equations (
26
) and
(
27
) would be analogous to those describing gradient flow in

[
18
]
. We note, however, that in our
settings of interest where
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a Poisson process
(
12
) or Gaussian diffusion process (
24
),

C_{f}^{t,s}
as defined by (
20
)
is not twice differentiable at the diagonal
s=t
, and
\{u^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}\sim\operatorname{GP}(0,C_{f})
does not admit a representation
\sqrt{\gamma}\,u^{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\bar{u}^{r}\mathrm{d}r
in the sense of a usual Lebesgue-Stieltjes integral, as it
does not have differentiable sample paths. Thus these
representations (
26
) and (
27
)
should be understood only in a formal sense.

One may also understand the linear operator
r_{f}^{t}(x^{[t]})

in (
17
) as an integrated linear response

(28)

r_{f}^{t}(x^{[t]})=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial w^{q}}\,x^{q}\mathrm{d}q

against the test process
x^{[t]}=\{x^{s}\}_{s\in[0,t]}
.
The evolution equation (
17
) is computed by formally
differentiating
f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)
in
w^{q}
using (
14
),

\frac{\partial f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial w^{q}}={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)\int_{q}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\bigg(\frac{\partial f(\xi^{r-},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial w^{q}}+\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{q-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathbf{1}_{r=q}\bigg)\mathrm{d}z^{r}.

To give a simple and mathematically rigorous meaning to the quantity

\int_{q}^{t}\mathbf{1}_{r=q}\mathrm{d}z^{r}
, we further integrate against
x^{q}\,\mathrm{d}q
and
exchange the orders of integration in
r
and
q
, yielding

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\partial f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial w^{q}}\,x^{q}\mathrm{d}q

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{r}\bigg(\frac{\partial f(\xi^{r-},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial w^{q}}+\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{q-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathbf{1}_{r=q}\bigg)x^{q}\mathrm{d}q\Bigg]\mathrm{d}z^{r}

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{t},w^{*},\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\Bigg[\int_{0}^{r}\frac{\partial f(\xi^{r},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial w^{q}}x^{q}\mathrm{d}q+\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\xi^{r-},w^{*},\varepsilon)x^{r}\Bigg]\mathrm{d}z^{r}.

This gives the equation (
17
) under the identification
(
28
).

We provide these remarks only to clarify a more intuitive interpretation of
these response processes; our mathematical proofs will operate directly on the
definitions (
13
–
23
),
rather than these formal interpretations.

2.3.3.
Existence and uniqueness of the DMFT fixed point

The following theorem establishes existence and
uniqueness of the above fixed points
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)

in a suitable domain
\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}\equiv\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}(T,C_{0})
.
We defer a precise definition of this
domain to Section
3
.

Theorem 2.5
.

Fix any large enough constant
C_{0}\equiv C_{0}(T)>0
, and let

\mathcal{S}\equiv\mathcal{S}(T,C_{0})
and

\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}\equiv\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}(T,C_{0})
be the spaces for

(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)
given in
Definition
3.1
. In both settings of

\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
in (
12
) for SGD and
in (
24
) for SME, the following hold:

(a)

Given any
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)\in\mathcal{S}
, and given
the filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}_{t\geq 0},\mathbb{P})

containing
(\theta^{0},\theta^{*}),\varepsilon,\{u^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},(\{w^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},w^{*}),\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
defined by (
10
), (
11
),
and (
12
)/(
24
),
there exist unique
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted and càdlàg solutions to
(
13
) and (
15
) for each fixed
s\in[0,T]
,
and unique
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-adapted and càdlàg solutions to
(
14
), (
16
), and
(
17
) for each fixed deterministic input process

x\in L^{4}([0,T],\mathbb{R}^{k})
.

(b)

There exists a unique point
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}\subset\mathcal{S}
that satisfies the fixed-point conditions
(
18
–
23
) defined by the solutions of
part (a).

This theorem guarantees, in particular, that the processes

\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
and
\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
which
will characterize the high-dimensional limits of SGD and SME are well-defined.

2.4.
Convergence to the limit processes

The following theorem is the main result of this paper. Recalling the data
matrix

\mathbf{X}=[\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},

we show that over fixed time horizons
[0,T]
, finite-time marginals
of the empirical
distributions of coordinates of
(\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\bm{\theta}^{*})

and
(\{\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\mathbf{X}\bm{\theta}^{*},\bm{\varepsilon})
for both the rescaled SGD
process (
7
) and the SME process (
8
)
converge in law to the preceding DMFT processes
\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
and

\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
,
defined via the fixed point
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}\equiv\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}(T,C_{0})
.

Theorem 2.6
.

Fix a time horizon
T>0
and
0\leq t_{1}\leq\ldots\leq t_{m}\leq T

not depending on
n,d
. For any sufficiently large constant
C_{0}>0
, let

\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}\equiv\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}(T,C_{0})
be the domain of Theorem

2.5
.

(a)

Let
\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}

be the time-rescaled SGD process (
7
)
where
\bm{\theta}^{t}=[\theta_{1}^{t},\ldots,\theta_{d}^{t}]^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
,
and let
\operatorname{Law}(\cdot)
denote the joint law of

(\{\theta^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},\theta^{*})
or

(\{\xi^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},w^{*},\varepsilon)
in the DMFT
system (
10
–
23
) when
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is the Poisson process
in (
12
),
defined by the unique fixed point
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)\in\mathcal{S}^{\text{cont}}
. Then almost surely as
n,d\to\infty
,
weakly and in Wasserstein-2 over
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}
and
(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}\times\mathbb{R}
,

(29)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\delta_{(\theta_{j}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta_{j}^{t_{m}},\theta_{j}^{*})}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\operatorname{Law}(\theta^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta^{t_{m}},\theta^{*}),

(30)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta_{(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t_{m}},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i})}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\operatorname{Law}(\xi^{t_{1}},\ldots,\xi^{t_{m}},w^{*},\varepsilon).

(b)

Let
\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
be the SME process
(
8
). Then the same result of part (a) holds, upon replacing
the DMFT system (
10
–
23
) by that
in the Gaussian setting of
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
given in (
24
).

Remark 2.7
.

Under Assumption
2.1
, the characterization of the limiting law for SGD
in Section
2.3.1
depends only on

(\bar{\kappa},\{\bar{\eta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0})
and not on
\alpha
. Thus the
high-dimensional limit of the SGD dynamics in Theorem
2.6
(a)
is the same for all
\alpha\in[0,1)
.

Remark 2.8
.

Theorem
2.6
implies, as a consequence, that for any
pseudo-Lipschitz test functions

\psi:(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}\to\mathbb{R}

and
\phi:(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}\times\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}

(i.e. satisfying

|\psi(x)-\psi(y)|\leq C\|x-y\|_{2}(1+\|x\|_{2}+\|y\|_{2})
for all inputs
x,y\in(\mathbb{R}^{k})^{m}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}
), almost surely

\displaystyle\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\psi(\theta_{j}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta_{j}^{t_{m}},\theta_{j}^{*})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\,\psi(\theta^{t_{1}},\ldots,\theta^{t_{m}},\theta^{*}),

\displaystyle\lim_{n,d\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t_{1}},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t_{m}},\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon_{i})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\,\phi(\xi^{t_{1}},\ldots,\xi^{t_{m}},w^{*},\varepsilon),

where the expectations on the right side are evaluated over the joint laws of
the DMFT variables.

For example, for any fixed training time
t>0
(in units of training epochs for
SGD, c.f. the time rescaling (
7
)), almost surely as
n,d\to\infty
:

(1)

The pairwise overlaps between columns of the learned parameter

\bm{\theta}^{t}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k}
and true parameter

\bm{\theta}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times k^{*}}
satisfy

\frac{1}{d}\,\bm{\theta}^{t\top}\bm{\theta}^{*}\to\mathbb{E}[\theta^{t}\otimes\theta^{*}]\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k^{*}}.

(2)

The average training loss satisfies

\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}L\big(\sigma(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t},y_{i})\big)\to\mathbb{E}L\big(\sigma(\xi^{t}),\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon))\big).

(3)

Suppose further that the data
\mathbf{x}_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{d}\,\mathrm{Id})
is Gaussian. Then for an
independent test
sample
\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{test}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{1}{d}\,\mathrm{Id})
equal in law to
\mathbf{x}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{x}_{n}
, conditional on
(\bm{\theta}^{t},\bm{\theta}^{*})
we have

(\mathbf{x}_{\text{test}}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{\text{test}}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*})\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{1}{d}(\bm{\theta}^{t},\bm{\theta}^{*})^{\top}(\bm{\theta}^{t},\bm{\theta}^{*})\right).

Thus for any test loss
L_{\text{test}}:\mathbb{R}^{k}\times\mathbb{R}^{k^{*}}\to\mathbb{R}
, the expected
test error admits a representation

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{test}}}L_{\text{test}}(\mathbf{x}_{\text{test}}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{\text{test}}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*})=h\left(\frac{1}{d}(\bm{\theta}^{t},\bm{\theta}^{*})^{\top}(\bm{\theta}^{t},\bm{\theta}^{*})\right)

for some smooth function
h:\mathbb{R}^{(k+k^{*})\times(k+k^{*})}\to\mathbb{R}
. Then also

\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}_{\mathrm{test}}}L_{\text{test}}(\mathbf{x}_{\text{test}}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t},\mathbf{x}_{\text{test}}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*})\to h\big(\mathbb{E}[(\theta,\theta^{*})\otimes(\theta,\theta^{*})]\big).

2.5.
Discussion

In this section, to provide further interpretation of our results and some
connections to related literature, we give a (heuristic) discussion of some
simplifications of the preceding DMFT
equations in the limit of small learning rate, limit of large sample size,
and specialization to a setting of quadratic optimization for squared loss and
ridge regularizer.

2.5.1.
Small learning rate limit and gradient flow

Consider, for simplicity, a time-independent learning rate

\bar{\eta}^{r}=\bar{\eta}
.
We check that in the small learning rate limit
\bar{\eta}\to 0
,
the DMFT system (
13
–
23
) converges to
that obtained for gradient flow in
[
18
]
.

Introduce the rescaled time
\tau=\bar{\eta}t
,
and define the time-rescaled processes

(31)

\begin{gathered}\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}=\theta^{\tau/\bar{\eta}},\quad\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}=\xi^{\tau/\bar{\eta}},\quad\tilde{u}^{\tau}=u^{\tau/\bar{\eta}},\quad\tilde{w}^{\tau}=w^{\tau/\bar{\eta}},\quad\tilde{z}^{\tau}=z^{\tau/\bar{\eta}},\\
\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{\tau,\sigma}=r_{\theta}^{\tau/\bar{\eta},\sigma/\bar{\eta}},\quad\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau,*}=r_{f}^{\tau/\bar{\eta},*},\quad\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau}(\tilde{x}^{[\tau]})=r_{f}^{\tau/\bar{\eta}}(x^{[\tau/\bar{\eta}]})\text{ where }\tilde{x}^{\tau}=x^{\tau/\bar{\eta}},\end{gathered}

together with the corresponding deterministic response and covariance processes

(32)

\begin{gathered}\tilde{C}_{\theta}^{\tau,\sigma}=C_{\theta}^{\tau/\bar{\eta},\sigma/\bar{\eta}},\quad\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\sigma}=R_{\theta}^{\tau/\bar{\eta},\sigma/\bar{\eta}},\quad\tilde{C}_{f}^{\tau,\sigma}=C_{f}^{\tau/\bar{\eta},\sigma/\bar{\eta}},\\
\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau}(x^{[\tau]})=R_{f}^{\tau/\bar{\eta}}(x^{[\tau/\bar{\eta}]}),\quad\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau,*}=R_{f}^{\tau/\bar{\eta},*},\quad\tilde{\Gamma}^{\tau}=\Gamma^{\tau/\bar{\eta}}.\end{gathered}

Note that
\{\tilde{u}^{\tau}\}\sim\operatorname{GP}(0,\tilde{C}_{f})
,

(\{\tilde{w}^{\tau}\},w^{*})\sim\operatorname{GP}(0,\tilde{C}_{\theta})
, and
\{\tilde{z}^{\tau}\}

is a Poisson process with rate
\bar{\kappa}/\bar{\eta}
in the SGD setting or a
diffusion process
\tilde{z}^{\tau}=\int_{0}^{\tau}(\bar{\kappa}/\bar{\eta})\mathrm{d}\rho+\int_{0}^{\tau}\sqrt{\bar{\kappa}/\bar{\eta}}\,\mathrm{d}b^{\rho}
in the SME setting.

Applying this change of variables to
(
13
–
17
), we have

(33)

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\theta^{0}-\int_{0}^{\tau}\Big({\gamma}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}\tilde{\theta}^{\rho}+g(\tilde{\theta}^{\rho})+{\gamma}\tilde{R}_{f}^{\rho}(\tilde{\theta}^{[\rho]})+{\gamma}\tilde{R}_{f}^{\rho,*}\theta^{*}\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho+\sqrt{\gamma}\,\tilde{u}^{\tau},

(34)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{\tau,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{k}-\int_{\sigma}^{\tau}\Big[\Big({\gamma}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}+\mathrm{D}g(\tilde{\theta}^{\rho})\Big)\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{\rho,\sigma}+\gamma\tilde{R}_{f}^{\rho}(\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{[\rho],\sigma})\Big]\mathrm{d}\rho\text{ if }\tau\geq\sigma,\quad\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{\tau,\sigma}=0\text{ if }\tau<\sigma,

(35)

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}

\displaystyle={-}\frac{\bar{\eta}}{\bar{\kappa}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}+\tilde{w}^{\tau},

(36)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau,*}

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)\frac{\bar{\eta}}{\bar{\kappa}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}\tilde{r}_{f}^{\rho-,*}\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}+\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon),

(37)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau}(\tilde{x}^{[\tau]})

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)\frac{\bar{\eta}}{\bar{\kappa}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}\Big(\tilde{r}_{f}^{\rho-}(\tilde{x}^{[\rho]})+\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\tilde{x}^{\rho}\Big)\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}.

From this, it may be verified that
\tilde{R}_{\theta},\tilde{R}_{f},\tilde{R}_{f}^{*}

remain uniformly bounded over finite intervals of rescaled time

\tau,\sigma\in[0,\tilde{T}]
, as
\bar{\eta}\to 0
. Then, decomposing
the evolution of
\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}
as

\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}={-}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}\rho-\underbrace{\frac{\bar{\eta}}{\bar{\kappa}}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho-},w^{*},\varepsilon)(\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}-(\bar{\kappa}/\bar{\eta})\mathrm{d}\rho)}_{:=M^{\tau}}+\,\tilde{w}^{\tau},

we note that the martingale term
M^{\tau}
satisfies

\mathbb{E}\|M^{\tau}\|^{2}\lesssim(\bar{\eta}/\bar{\kappa})^{2}\cdot(\bar{\kappa}/\bar{\eta})=O(\bar{\eta})

over finite time horizons
\tau\in[0,\tilde{T}]
,
by the Itô isometry. Then a
high-moment bound may be applied to show

\lim_{\bar{\eta}\to 0}\sup_{\tau\in[0,\tilde{T}]}\|M^{\tau}\|=0
. The
same argument establishes that the martingale terms for the evolutions of

\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau,*}
and
\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau}(\tilde{x}^{[\tau]})
vanish in the
limit
\bar{\eta}\to 0
, leading to the simplified equations

(38)

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}

\displaystyle={-}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}\rho+\tilde{w}^{\tau},

(39)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau,*}

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}\tilde{r}_{f}^{\rho,*}\mathrm{d}\rho+\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon),

(40)

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau}(\tilde{x}^{[\tau]})

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}\Big(\tilde{r}_{f}^{\rho}(\tilde{x}^{[\rho]})+\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho},w^{*},\varepsilon)\tilde{x}^{\rho}\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho.

Positing the form

\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau}(\tilde{x}^{[\tau]})=\int_{0}^{\tau}\frac{\partial f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial\tilde{w}^{\sigma}}\,\tilde{x}^{\sigma}\mathrm{d}\sigma

as in Remark
2.4
, this response process

\frac{\partial f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial\tilde{w}^{\sigma}}
must then
satisfy

(41)

\frac{\partial f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial\tilde{w}^{\sigma}}={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)\left(\int_{\sigma}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}\frac{\partial f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho},w^{*},\varepsilon)}{\partial\tilde{w}^{\sigma}}\mathrm{d}\rho+\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\sigma}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\sigma},w^{*},\varepsilon)\right).

These equations (
33
), (
34
), (
38
),
(
39
), and (
41
) (specialized to the setting of a
ridge-type regularizer
G(\theta)=\frac{1}{2}\theta^{\top}\Lambda\theta
)
are equivalent to the DMFT system established in
[
18
]

for gradient flow.

2.5.2.
Large-sample limit and one-pass SGD

Recalling the parameter
\gamma=\lim_{n,d\to\infty}n/d
, we next consider a
data-rich regime where
\gamma\to\infty
. For simplicity,
consider a constant learning rate
\eta^{t}=\bar{\eta}^{t}=\bar{\eta}

and single-sample batch size
\kappa=\bar{\kappa}=1
.
To keep the stochastic gradient dynamics (
6
) the same as
n
grows
(i.e. depending on
d
rather than
n
), we define

\tilde{g}(\theta)=\gamma^{-1}g(\theta)

and fix
\tilde{g}(\cdot)
as
\gamma\to\infty
.
Rescaling time as
\tau=\gamma t
, let us show that over any fixed time horizon

\tau\in[0,\tilde{T}]
(corresponding to a number of SGD iterations
n(\tilde{T}/\gamma)\approx d\tilde{T}
) that
does not depend on
n
), the preceding DMFT equations
reduce to a simple diffusion process that characterizes the one-pass or online
SGD procedure

\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{k+1}=\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{k}-\bar{\eta}\Big(\mathbf{x}^{k}\otimes f(\mathbf{x}^{k\top}\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{k},\mathbf{x}^{k\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon^{k})+\frac{1}{d}\,\tilde{g}(\bar{\bm{\theta}}^{k})\Big)

with a fresh sample
(\mathbf{x}^{k},y^{k})=(\mathbf{x}^{k},\sigma^{*}(\mathbf{x}^{k\top}\bm{\theta}^{*},\varepsilon^{k}))

per iteration. We note that this is the standard one-pass SGD procedure for
minimizing the population risk

\mathbb{E}[L\big(\sigma(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta}),y\big)]+\frac{1}{d}\sum_{j=1}^{d}\tilde{G}(\theta_{j}),\qquad\tilde{G}(\theta)=\gamma^{-1}G(\theta).

We define the time-rescaled processes
\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}=\theta^{\tau/\gamma}
,

\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}=\xi^{\tau/\gamma}
,
\tilde{z}^{\tau}=z^{\tau/\gamma}
,
etc. analogously to
(
31
–
32
). Then applying this change of variables
to (
13
–
17
),

\displaystyle\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}

\displaystyle=\theta^{0}-\bar{\eta}\int_{0}^{\tau}\Big(\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}\tilde{\theta}^{\rho}+\tilde{g}(\tilde{\theta}^{\rho})+\tilde{R}_{f}^{\rho}(\tilde{\theta}^{[\rho]})+\tilde{R}_{f}^{\rho,*}\theta^{*}\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho+\sqrt{\gamma}\,\tilde{u}^{\tau},

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{\tau,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Id}_{k}-\bar{\eta}\int_{\sigma}^{\tau}\Big[\Big(\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}+\mathrm{D}g(\tilde{\theta}^{\rho})\Big)\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{\rho,\sigma}+\tilde{R}_{f}^{\rho}(\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{[\rho],\sigma})\Big]\mathrm{d}\rho\text{ if }\tau\geq\sigma,\quad\tilde{r}_{\theta}^{\tau,\sigma}=0\text{ if }\tau<\sigma,

\displaystyle\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}

\displaystyle={-}\bar{\eta}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}+\tilde{w}^{\tau},

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau,*}

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)\cdot\bar{\eta}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}\tilde{r}_{f}^{\rho-,*}\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}+\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon),

\displaystyle\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau}(\tilde{x}^{[\tau]})

\displaystyle={-}\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)\cdot\bar{\eta}\int_{0}^{\tau}\tilde{R}_{\theta}^{\tau,\rho}\Big(\tilde{r}_{f}^{\rho-}(\tilde{x}^{[\rho]})+\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\tilde{x}^{\rho}\Big)\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}.

Here,
\{\tilde{z}^{\tau}\}_{\tau\geq 0}
is a Poisson process with rate

1/\gamma
in the setting of SGD, or the diffusion process
\tilde{z}^{\tau}=\int_{0}^{\tau}(1/\gamma)\mathrm{d}\rho+\int_{0}^{\tau}(1/\sqrt{\gamma})\mathrm{d}b^{\rho}

in the setting of SME. Then one may again verify that
\tilde{R}_{\theta},\tilde{R}_{f},\tilde{R}_{f}^{*}
remain uniformly bounded as
\gamma\to\infty
, and that in this limit, all stochastic integrals against
\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}
vanish to give the simplified equations

(42)

\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}=\theta^{0}-\bar{\eta}\int_{0}^{\tau}\Big(\tilde{\Gamma}^{\rho}\tilde{\theta}^{\rho}+\tilde{g}(\tilde{\theta}^{\rho})+\tilde{R}_{f}^{\rho,*}\theta^{*}\Big)\mathrm{d}\rho+\sqrt{\gamma}\,\tilde{u}^{\tau},

\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}=\tilde{w}^{\tau},\quad\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau,*}=\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)=\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}f(\tilde{w}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon),\quad\tilde{r}_{f}^{\tau}\equiv 0.

The covariance kernel of the rescaled Gaussian process
\{\sqrt{\gamma}\,\tilde{u}^{\tau}\}_{\tau\geq 0}
is given by

\displaystyle\gamma\,\tilde{C}_{f}^{\tau,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\gamma\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\bar{\eta}\int_{0}^{\tau}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}\otimes\bar{\eta}\int_{0}^{\sigma}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho-},w^{*},\varepsilon)\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{\rho}\Big].

Applying the Itô isometry and above simplification

\tilde{\xi}^{\tau}=\tilde{w}^{\tau}
in the
\gamma\to\infty
limit,
we then obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{\gamma\to\infty}\gamma\,\tilde{C}_{f}^{\tau,\sigma}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\gamma\to\infty}\left(O\Big(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}\Big)+\gamma\bar{\eta}^{2}\,\mathbb{E}\Big[\Big(\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma}f(\tilde{\xi}^{\rho-},w^{*},\varepsilon)(\mathrm{d}\tilde{z}^{r}-(1/\gamma)\mathrm{d}r)\Big)^{\otimes 2}\Big]\right)

\displaystyle=\bar{\eta}^{2}\int_{0}^{\tau\wedge\sigma}\mathbb{E}\Big[f(\tilde{w}^{\rho},w^{*},\varepsilon)^{\otimes 2}\Big]\mathrm{d}\rho.

This is precisely the covariance kernel of a Brownian diffusion

\mathrm{d}\tilde{u}^{\tau}=\bar{\eta}\Sigma^{\tau}\mathrm{d}B^{\tau},\qquad\Sigma^{\tau}=\mathbb{E}[f(\tilde{w}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)^{\otimes 2}]^{1/2},

where
\{B^{\tau}\}_{\tau\geq 0}
is a standard Brownian motion on
\mathbb{R}^{k}
.

Thus, to summarize, in the limit
\gamma\to\infty
,
the law of
\{\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}\}_{\tau\geq 0}
in (
42
)
is given by a distribution-dependent SDE

(43)

\mathrm{d}\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}={-}\bar{\eta}\Big(\tilde{\Gamma}^{\tau}\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}+\tilde{g}(\tilde{\theta}^{\tau})+\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau,*}\theta^{*}\Big)\mathrm{d}\tau+\bar{\eta}\,\Sigma^{\tau}\mathrm{d}B^{\tau},

where the deterministic drift and diffusion
coefficients
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\tau},\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau,*},\Sigma^{\tau}
are
defined self-consistently from the law of
(\tilde{\theta}^{\tau},\theta^{*})
by

(44)

\begin{gathered}\tilde{C}_{\theta}=\mathbb{E}[(\tilde{\theta}^{\tau},\theta^{*})\otimes(\tilde{\theta}^{\tau},\theta^{*})]\in\mathbb{R}^{(k+k^{*})\times(k+k^{*})},\quad(\tilde{w}^{\tau},w^{*})\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\tilde{C}_{\theta}),\\
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\tau}=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{D}_{\xi}f(\tilde{w}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)],\quad\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau,*}=\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}f(\tilde{w}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)],\quad\Sigma^{\tau}=\mathbb{E}[f(\tilde{w}^{\tau},w^{*},\varepsilon)^{\otimes 2}]^{1/2}.\end{gathered}

This coincides with the high-dimensional limit of the
learning dynamics of one-pass SGD. For example, specializing to the case of a
ridge penalty
\tilde{G}(\theta)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\theta\|^{2}
and

\tilde{g}(\theta)=\lambda\theta
, we may apply Itô’s formula to
(
43
) to obtain a closed system of ODEs for the overlap
parameters
\tilde{C}_{\theta}=\mathbb{E}[(\tilde{\theta}^{\tau},\theta^{*})\otimes(\tilde{\theta}^{\tau},\theta^{*})]
, which take the form

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}\otimes\theta^{*}]

\displaystyle=-\bar{\eta}\left((\tilde{\Gamma}^{\tau}+\lambda)\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}\otimes\theta^{*}]+\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau,*}\mathbb{E}[\theta^{*}\otimes\theta^{*}]\right),

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}\otimes\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}]

\displaystyle=-\bar{\eta}\bigg((\tilde{\Gamma}^{\tau}+\lambda)\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}\otimes\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}]+\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau,*}\mathbb{E}[\theta^{*}\otimes\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}]

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}\otimes\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}](\tilde{\Gamma}^{\tau}+\lambda)^{\top}+\mathbb{E}[\tilde{\theta}^{\tau}\otimes\theta^{*}]\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau,*\top}\bigg)+\bar{\eta}^{2}(\Sigma^{\tau})^{2}

and
\tilde{\Gamma}^{\tau},\tilde{R}_{f}^{\tau,*},\Sigma^{\tau}
are the functions of

\tilde{C}_{\theta}
defined by (
44
). This recovers the known ODE
for overlap parameters in one-pass SGD (in a setting of isotropic data), see
e.g.
[
22
, Corollary 1.1]
.

2.5.3.
Deterministic integro-differential equations for squared loss and
ridge regularizer

We next consider a special setting of quadratic optimization, given by a
squared loss, linear activation, and ridge regularizer.
2
2

2

The result we establish does not
technically encompass this setting, as Assumption
2.3

requires both
L(\cdot,\cdot)
and
\sigma(\cdot)
to be Lipschitz. However, we
include this discussion here, as we believe it is illuminating to understand
the simplifications that arise in this linear example.
In this setting,
the DMFT equations become linear, and the relevant mean and covariance
statistics of

\{\theta^{t},\xi^{t},r_{\theta}^{t,s},r_{f}^{t},r_{f}^{t,*}\}
may be calculated
analytically to yield closed integro-differential equations for the two mappings

(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})\mapsto(C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma),\qquad(C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)\mapsto(C_{\theta},R_{\theta}).

A corollary of this calculation is that, in this linear setting, the fixed point

(C_{\theta},R_{\theta},C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)
depends only on the mean and
covariance of
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
, and hence coincides for SGD and SME. (More generally, this fixed point coincides for the squared loss, linear activation, and any regularizer, as the mapping
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})\mapsto(C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)
is always the same for both SGD and SME.)

Specifically, let
k=k^{*}
and consider the setting

L(\hat{y},y)=\frac{1}{2}\|\hat{y}-y\|^{2},\qquad\sigma(\xi)=\xi,\qquad f(\xi,w^{*},\varepsilon)=\xi-\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon).

Let us denote

\bar{\xi}^{t}=\xi^{t}-\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon),\qquad\bar{w}^{t}=w^{t}-\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon),\qquad\bar{z}^{t}=z^{t}-\bar{\kappa}\,t.

Then (
14
), (
16
), and
(
17
) simplify to

\displaystyle\bar{\xi}^{t}

\displaystyle={-}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}z^{r}+\bar{w}^{t}={-}\int_{0}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{\xi}^{r}\mathrm{d}r\underbrace{-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r}}_{:=M^{t}}+\,\bar{w}^{t},

\displaystyle r_{f}^{t,*}

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}r_{f}^{r-,*}\mathrm{d}z^{r}-\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon),

\displaystyle r_{f}^{t}(x^{[t]})

\displaystyle={-}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\big(r_{f}^{r-}(x^{[r]})+x^{r}\big)\mathrm{d}z^{r}.

Noting that
r_{f}^{r-}(x^{[r]})
and
r_{f}^{r-,*}
are
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-predictable,
taking expectations of the last two equations yields

\displaystyle R_{f}^{t,*}

\displaystyle={-}\int_{0}^{t}\bar{\eta}^{r}R_{\theta}^{t,r}R_{f}^{r,*}\mathrm{d}r-\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon)],

\displaystyle R_{f}^{t}(x^{[t]})

\displaystyle={-}\int_{0}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}\big(R_{f}^{r}(x^{[r]})+x^{r}\big)\mathrm{d}r.

These are Volterra integral equations of the second kind, and explicitly
solvable: Let
K_{\theta}^{t,s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}

denote the unique resolvent kernel (see e.g.
[
17
, Chap 2]
) satisfying

(45)

K_{\theta}^{t,s}=-{\bar{\eta}^{s}}R_{\theta}^{t,s}-\int_{s}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}K_{\theta}^{r,s}\mathrm{d}r={-}\bar{\eta}^{s}R_{\theta}^{t,s}-\int_{s}^{t}\bar{\eta}^{s}K_{\theta}^{t,r}R_{\theta}^{r,s}\mathrm{d}r.

Then the explicit solutions for
R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma
are

(46)

R_{f}^{t}(x^{[t]})=\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,s}x^{s}\mathrm{d}s,\quad R_{f}^{t,*}=-\bigg(1+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,s}\mathrm{d}s\bigg)\mathbb{E}[\mathrm{D}_{w^{*}}\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon)],\quad\Gamma^{t}=\mathrm{Id}_{k}.

To derive the form of
C_{f}
, note that the above Volterra integral equation
for
\bar{\xi}^{t}
may also be solved to yield

(47)

\bar{\xi}^{t}=M^{t}+\bar{w}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,s}(M^{s}+\bar{w}^{s})\mathrm{d}s.

We observe that since
\{\bar{z}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
is a mean-zero process
independent of
(\{w^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},w^{*},\varepsilon)
, for any
t,s\geq 0
this
implies

\mathbb{E}[M^{t}\otimes\bar{w}^{s}]=\mathbb{E}[\mathbb{E}[M^{t}\mid\{w^{t}\}_{t\in[0,T]},w^{*},\varepsilon]\otimes\bar{w}^{s}]=0
. Thus

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{\xi}^{t,s}:=\mathbb{E}[\bar{\xi}^{t}\otimes\bar{\xi}^{s}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(M^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,r}M^{r}\mathrm{d}r\right)\otimes\left(M^{s}+\int_{0}^{s}K_{\theta}^{s,r}M^{r}\mathrm{d}r\right)\right]

\displaystyle\hskip 72.26999pt+\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\bar{w}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{w}^{r}\mathrm{d}r\right)\otimes\left(\bar{w}^{s}+\int_{0}^{s}K_{\theta}^{s,r}\bar{w}^{r}\mathrm{d}r\right)\right].

Denote

(48)

\bar{C}_{\theta}^{t,s}=\mathbb{E}[\bar{w}^{t}\otimes\bar{w}^{s}]=\mathbb{E}[(w^{t}-\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon))\otimes(w^{s}-\sigma^{*}(w^{*},\varepsilon))],\quad(\{w^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},w^{*})\sim\operatorname{GP}(0,C_{\theta}).

Then the contribution from
\{\bar{w}^{t}\}
is

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({\bar{w}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{w}^{r}\mathrm{d}r}\right)\otimes\left({\bar{w}^{s}+\int_{0}^{s}K_{\theta}^{s,r^{\prime}}\bar{w}^{r^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\bar{C}_{\theta}^{t,s}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{C}_{\theta}^{r,s}\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{s}\bar{C}_{\theta}^{t,r^{\prime}}K_{\theta}^{s,r^{\prime}\top}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{C}_{\theta}^{r,r^{\prime}}K_{\theta}^{s,r^{\prime}\top}\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}.

For the contribution from
\{M^{t}\}
, observe that

\displaystyle M^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,r}M^{r}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{s}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,s}\bar{\xi}^{s-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{s}-\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,s}\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{s,r}\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r}\mathrm{d}s

(49)

\displaystyle=-\int_{0}^{t}\left({\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}R_{\theta}^{t,r}+\int_{r}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}K_{\theta}^{t,s}R_{\theta}^{s,r}\mathrm{d}s}\right)\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r}=\frac{1}{\bar{\kappa}}\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r}.

Then by the Itô isometry,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({M^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,r}M^{r}\mathrm{d}r}\right)\otimes\left({M^{s}+\int_{0}^{s}K_{\theta}^{s,r}M^{r}\mathrm{d}r}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{\kappa}}\int_{0}^{t\wedge s}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\mathbb{E}[\bar{\xi}^{r}\otimes\bar{\xi}^{r}]K_{\theta}^{s,r\top}\mathrm{d}r.

Combining these arguments, the covariance of
\{\bar{\xi}^{t}\}
satisfies

(50)

\displaystyle\bar{C}_{\xi}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\bar{\kappa}}\int_{0}^{t\wedge s}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r,r}K_{\theta}^{s,r\top}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle+\bar{C}_{\theta}^{t,s}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{C}_{\theta}^{r,s}\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{s}\bar{C}_{\theta}^{t,r^{\prime}}K_{\theta}^{s,r^{\prime}\top}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}K_{\theta}^{t,r}\bar{C}_{\theta}^{r,r^{\prime}}K_{\theta}^{s,r^{\prime}\top}\mathrm{d}r\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}.

This may be understood as a closed linear integral equation for the diagonal

\{\bar{C}_{\xi}^{t,t}\}_{t\in[0,T]}
, whose solution may then be
substituted back to determine
\{\bar{C}_{\xi}^{t,s}\}_{s\neq t}
.
Finally, by definition

\displaystyle C_{f}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({\int_{0}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}\bar{\xi}^{r}\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r}}\right)\otimes\left({\int_{0}^{s}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}\bar{\xi}^{r}\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r}}\right)}\right]

\displaystyle=(\mathrm{I})+(\mathrm{II})+(\mathrm{III})+(\mathrm{IV}),

where the terms are given by

\displaystyle(\mathrm{I})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\bar{\eta}^{r}\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}[\bar{\xi}^{r}\otimes\bar{\xi}^{r^{\prime}}]\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\bar{\eta}^{r}\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r,r^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}r^{\prime},

\displaystyle(\mathrm{II})

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[{\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r}\otimes\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{\xi}^{r^{\prime}-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r^{\prime}}}\right]=\int_{0}^{t\wedge s}\frac{(\bar{\eta}^{r})^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r,r}\mathrm{d}r,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{III})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\bar{\xi}^{r}\otimes\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{\xi}^{r^{\prime}-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r^{\prime}}}\right]\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\overset{(*)}{=}\int_{0}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\left({M^{r}+\int_{0}^{r}K_{\theta}^{r,r^{\prime}}M^{r^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}}\right)\otimes\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{\xi}^{r^{\prime}-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r^{\prime}}}\right]\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle\overset{(**)}{=}\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\int_{0}^{r}K_{\theta}^{r,r^{\prime}}\bar{\xi}^{r^{\prime}-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r^{\prime}}\otimes\int_{0}^{s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{\xi}^{r^{\prime}-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r^{\prime}}}\right]\mathrm{d}r=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{r\wedge s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}}{\bar{\kappa}}K_{\theta}^{r,r^{\prime}}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r^{\prime},r^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}{r^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}r,

\displaystyle(\mathrm{IV})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{s}\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}\mathbb{E}\left[{\int_{0}^{t}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{\xi}^{r-}\mathrm{d}\bar{z}^{r}\otimes\bar{\xi}^{r^{\prime}}}\right]\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{s}\int_{0}^{t\wedge r}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r^{\prime},r^{\prime}}K_{\theta}^{r,r^{\prime}\top}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\mathrm{d}r.

In
(*)
, we have applied the form of
\{x^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
from
(
47
), and the independence of
(\{\bar{w}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0},w^{*},\varepsilon)
and
\{\bar{z}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
as above. In
(**)
, we have applied
again the identity (
49
), and the form for
(\mathrm{IV})

follows from symmetric arguments as
(\mathrm{III})
. Thus, to summarize

(51)

\displaystyle C_{f}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}\bar{\eta}^{r}\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r,r^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}r\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}+\int_{0}^{t\wedge s}\frac{(\bar{\eta}^{r})^{2}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r,r}\mathrm{d}r

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{r\wedge s}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}}{\bar{\kappa}}K_{\theta}^{r,r^{\prime}}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r^{\prime},r^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}{r^{\prime}}\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{s}\int_{0}^{t\wedge r}\frac{\bar{\eta}^{r}\bar{\eta}^{r^{\prime}}}{\bar{\kappa}}\bar{C}_{\xi}^{r^{\prime},r^{\prime}}K_{\theta}^{r,r^{\prime}\top}\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\mathrm{d}r.

The mapping
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})\mapsto(C_{f},R_{f},R_{f}^{*},\Gamma)
is thus
given by (
45
),
(
46
), (
48
), (
50
),
and (
51
), and this mapping is the same for both SGD and SME.

Consider next the setting of a ridge regularizer,

G(\theta)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\|\theta\|^{2},\qquad g(\theta)=\lambda\theta,

and suppose that
R_{f}
takes the form

R_{f}^{t}(\theta^{[t]})=\int_{0}^{t}R_{f}^{t,s}\theta^{s}ds
.
Then (
13
) and (
15
) also simplify
to linear Volterra integral equations,

\theta^{t}=\int_{0}^{t}A_{f}^{t,r}\theta^{r}\mathrm{d}r+b_{f}^{t},\qquad r_{\theta}^{t,s}=\int_{s}^{t}A_{f}^{t,r}r_{\theta}^{r,s}\mathrm{d}r+\mathrm{Id}_{k},

where

\displaystyle A_{f}^{t,r}

\displaystyle=-\bar{\eta}^{r}\left({\gamma}\Gamma^{r}+\lambda\,\mathrm{Id}_{k}\right)-\gamma\int_{r}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{s}}R_{f}^{r,s}\mathrm{d}s,\qquad b_{f}^{t}=\theta^{0}-\gamma\left(\int_{0}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}R_{f}^{r,*}\mathrm{d}r\right)\theta^{*}+\sqrt{\gamma}\,u^{t}.

Let
K_{f}^{t,s}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times k}
be the unique resolvent kernel satisfying

(52)

K_{f}^{t,s}=A_{f}^{t,s}+\int_{s}^{t}A_{f}^{t,r}K_{f}^{r,s}\mathrm{d}r=A_{f}^{t,s}+\int_{s}^{t}K_{f}^{t,r}A_{f}^{r,s}\mathrm{d}s.

Then

(53)

R_{\theta}^{t,s}=\mathbb{E}r_{\theta}^{t,s}=\left(1+\int_{s}^{t}K_{f}^{t,r}\mathrm{d}r\right)\mathrm{Id}_{k},

and

\theta^{t}=b_{f}^{t}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{f}^{t,s}b_{f}^{s}\,\mathrm{d}s

which implies that
C_{\theta}^{t,s}=\mathbb{E}[\theta^{t}\otimes\theta^{s}]
is given by

(54)

\displaystyle C_{\theta}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=C_{b}^{t,s}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{f}^{t,r}C_{b}^{r,s}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{s}C_{b}^{t,r}(K_{f}^{s,r})^{\top}\,\mathrm{d}r+\int_{0}^{t}\int_{0}^{s}K_{f}^{t,r}C_{b}^{r,r^{\prime}}(K_{f}^{s,r^{\prime}})^{\top}\,\mathrm{d}r^{\prime}\,\mathrm{d}r,

\displaystyle C_{\theta}^{t,*}

\displaystyle=C_{b}^{t,*}+\int_{0}^{t}K_{f}^{t,s}C_{b}^{s,*}\mathrm{d}s,

\displaystyle C_{b}^{t,s}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[b_{f}^{t}\otimes b_{f}^{s}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\theta^{0}-\gamma\left(\int_{0}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}R_{f}^{r,*}\mathrm{d}r\right)\theta^{*}\right)\otimes\left(\theta^{0}-\gamma\left(\int_{0}^{s}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}R_{f}^{r,*}\mathrm{d}r\right)\theta^{*}\right)\right]+\gamma\,C_{f}^{t,s},

\displaystyle C_{b}^{t,*}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[b_{f}^{t}\otimes\theta^{*}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\theta^{0}-\gamma\left(\int_{0}^{t}{\bar{\eta}^{r}}R_{f}^{r,*}\mathrm{d}r\right)\theta^{*}\right)\otimes\theta^{*}\right].

The mapping
(C_{f},R_{f},R^{*}_{f},\Gamma)\mapsto(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})
is given
by (
52
), (
53
), and
(
54
).

2.5.4.
Numerical simulation of the DMFT system

Figure 1.
Comparison of SGD and SME dynamics in a linear model.
Parameters:
n=8000
,
d=10000
,
\eta=0.8
,
\lambda=0.1
. The dynamics of the
squared norm
\|\bm{\theta}_{t}\|^{2}/d
, overlap
\langle\bm{\theta}_{t},\bm{\theta}^{*}\rangle/d
, and training loss agree closely for SGD and SME, whereas those of
the empirical CDF
n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathbf{1}\{|\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}|\leq 1\}
show a
discrepancy.

Figure 2.
Divergence of SGD and SME dynamics in a model with Huber loss and

\tanh
activation.
Parameters:
n=8000
,
d=10000
,
\eta=3.0
,
\lambda=0.1
. Solid/dotted curves
depict the dynamics of SGD/SME and their DMFT predictions over 4
training epochs, exhibiting a divergence that is predicted by DMFT.
Dashed curves for training times
t>4
depict the subsequent dynamics of
gradient flow initialized from the last SGD/SME iterate, in which the norm
and overlap statistics re-converge.

Figure 3.
Divergence of SGD and SME dynamics in a model with Huber loss and

\sin
activation. Parameters are the same as Figure
2
. In this
example, the difference between SGD and SME persists through the later gradient
flow training, suggesting that the dynamics reach local optima
with distinct statistical properties.

We simulate the solution to the DMFT
system (
10
–
23
) by iterating the fixed-point
mappings
(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})\mapsto(C_{f},R_{f},R^{*}_{f},\Gamma)

and
(C_{f},R_{f},R^{*}_{f},\Gamma)\mapsto(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})
until convergence, and
then simulating the processes
\{\theta^{t},\xi^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
from the
parameters of the fixed point. More concretely:

•

In the setting of a ridge regularizer, the mapping

(C_{f},R_{f},R^{*}_{f},\Gamma)\mapsto(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})
may be evaluated
analytically via (
52
), (
53
), and
(
54
).

•

In the setting of squared loss and linear activation, the mapping

(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})\mapsto(C_{f},R_{f},R^{*}_{f},\Gamma)
may likewise be evaluated
analytically via (
45
),
(
46
), (
48
), (
50
),
and (
51
).

•

Outside of these settings, the expectations
(
18
–
23
) may be approximated
via Monte Carlo simulation of the processes
(
10
–
17
).

All time integrals are discretized in increments of
\delta
, as described in
Section
4.1
. We remark that simulation of the processes

\{\xi^{t},r_{f}^{t,*},r_{f}^{t,s}\}
for SGD is substantially faster
than for gradient flow or SME, as integrals against the Poisson
process
\{z^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
are rapidly computable due to the sparsity of its
jumps. A software implementation of these methods is available on the authors’
web page.

We close this section by presenting some numerical results that corroborate our
preceding discussion/findings. All simulations use a single-index model

k=k^{*}=1
, single-sample batch size
\kappa=1
, time discretization
\delta=0.05
,
and ridge regularizer
G(\theta)=\frac{\lambda}{2}\theta^{2}
with
analytical computation of the DMFT mapping

(C_{f},R_{f},R^{*}_{f},\Gamma)\mapsto(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})
. The reverse DMFT mapping
(C_{f},R_{f},R^{*}_{f},\Gamma)\mapsto(C_{\theta},R_{\theta})
is
computed analytically for the linear model, and via 10,000 Monte Carlo samples
otherwise. DMFT predictions for
d^{-1}\|\bm{\theta}^{t}\|^{2}
and
d^{-1}\bm{\theta}^{t\top}\bm{\theta}^{*}
are contained in
C_{\theta}
, and the predictions for other observables are also computed via 10,000 Monte Carlo samples. Statistics of the high-dimensional SGD and SME processes

\{\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{t\geq 0}
are averaged over 10 independent trials.

Coincidence of quadratic observables of SGD and SME in a linear model.

For a linear model
y=\mathbf{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*}
with loss
L(\hat{y},y)=\frac{1}{2}(\hat{y}-y)^{2}
and activation
\sigma(\xi)=\xi
,
Figure
1
verifies the claim of Section
2.5.3

that the dynamics of the squared norm

d^{-1}\|\bm{\theta}^{t}\|^{2}
, overlap
d^{-1}\bm{\theta}^{t\top}\bm{\theta}^{*}
,
and training loss
(2n)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t}-\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*})^{2}
coincide for SGD and SME. The empirical distribution of
\{\mathbf{x}_{i}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{t}\}_{i\in[n]}
is predicted (via the law of
\xi^{t}
) to be different
for SGD and SME, and we indeed observe a discrepancy at the tested
learning rate that is also predicted by the DMFT theory.

Difference between SGD and SME dynamics in non-convex landscapes.

Figures
2
and
3
display two different non-linear
and non-convex settings, in which the dynamics of SGD and SME
with relatively large learning rate
\eta=3.0
are markedly different:

(1)

Figure
2
uses a (Lipschitz-continuous) Huber loss

L(\hat{y},y)=\begin{cases}\frac{1}{2}(\hat{y}-y)^{2}&\text{if }|\hat{y}-y|<1\\
(|\hat{y}-y|-\frac{1}{2})&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}
and activation
\sigma(x)=\tanh(x)
, with noisy true labels
y=\tanh(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\bm{\theta}^{*}+\varepsilon)
where
\varepsilon\sim\mathcal{N}(0,0.1)
.

(2)

Figure
3
uses the same Huber loss, activation

\sigma(x)=\sin(x)
, and noisy true labels
